[06](#03_toc01.xhtml#fm_toc_045)

Since the publication of the book in May 2019, Deliver has gone through an extensive transformation, including two of the founding companies leaving the consortium (ABN-AMRO and Samsung SDS). With only the Port of Rotterdam left, the question was very much if and how to continue with Deliver. However, before we dive deeper into the vision, principles and architecture of what is now called the Naviporta,^([1](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en1)) we briefly describe the course of events between February 2019 and December 2021.

In February 2019 the consortium completed a proof of concept (PoC) in which a shipping container was successfully tracked and traced door to door and instantly financed between the Port of Busan and the warehouse of Samsung SDS in Tilburg, the Netherlands (Port of Rotterdam, 2019). This PoC provided proof that interoperability could be achieved between two different blockchain ecosystems operating different blockchain protocols, using the Ethereum public blockchain as a common frame of reference.

This PoC was extended to include other trade lanes. Various export shipments from China as well as the USA into the Netherlands were trailed between May 2019 and April 2020. Also, the functionality of the original PoC was further extended to allow for these additional pilots; however, from a technical perspective the focus was on finalizing the future state architecture (FSA) of the first enterprise-ready release of the platform. The main objective of this extended pilot period was to show proof of value (PoV). In particular, the focus was on identifying the business case for the Deliver platform as well as the producers and consumers on the platform. This was done by doing extensive analysis of existing processes, in particular shipment processing times and costs, and costs of non-conformance and non-compliance. This approach was firmly rooted in the framework as proposed in [Chapter 2](#05_chapter02.xhtml#ch02_sec_001) of this book. While at the end of this phase proof of value had been successfully demonstrated, certain factors external to the project had considerably changed for two of the consortium members, ABN-AMRO and Samsung SDS. For the former, the decision was triggered by the collapse of several commodity traders, after instances of fraud were discovered following the coronavirus crisis (Reuters, 2020), and resulted in ABN-AMRO exiting from commodity and trade finance altogether, which was the intended use of the platform. Samsung SDS’s decision to exit was a result of a strategic reorientation prioritizing the further development of its Cello platform.

## [The case for the digital quayside](#03_toc01.xhtml#fm_toc_046)

The decision to quit by ABN-AMRO and Samsung SDS made Port of Rotterdam (PoR) the caretaker of the Deliver legacy. Not one but two out of the three consortium members bailing out obviously left the port with the question why they should develop and maintain such an infrastructure.

The answer can be found in inter-port competition, that is competition between different ports serving the same supply chains and/or hinterland. Physical connectivity to the hinterland is an important competitiveness criterion (Parola F, 2017) and ports should be able to seamlessly integrate various logistics chains and processes that pass through the port. This requires collaboration and coordination that often does not arise spontaneously (Van der Horst M, 2008). The logjams at LA’s ports are a painful reminder of what happens when parties with opposing interests that do not trust one another, or thrive on information asymmetry, must coordinate their activities (Henry, 2021). Therefore, a case can be made for a digital infrastructure-as-a-service (IaaS) that ensures seamless connectivity with overseas ports, members of the port community and the hinterland. As such it can be thought of as an extension of the physical port infrastructure, a digital quayside, that facilitates the sharing of data and assets between siloed systems of record on which third-party vendors can deploy their services. Ideally such an infrastructure should be governed independently and not be considered a dominant member in the supply chain nor should it have any direct financial interest in any of the services deployed on the infrastructure. Landlord ports such as the PoR already play a similar role when it comes to the physical infrastructure. They offer the infrastructure (eg quaysides, land, roads) but not the services (eg transport, freight forwarding, container terminal). Given that collaboration and coordination between the various supply chain members can only be accomplished by sharing data, extending that role to the digital realm is therefore a logical next step. Naviporta’s governance structure is described in more detail in the ‘Governance’ section below.

## [Lessons learned](#03_toc01.xhtml#fm_toc_047)

With hindsight always comes 20-20 vision and with Naviporta (Port of Rotterdam, 2021) now live there are some valuable lessons to be learned. Not only things we would do differently, but also things we would do more, or have proven to be decisive in getting from idea to realization.

### Scale, but not at all costs

While we concur that initial scaling of the solution would have happened much faster by having several large shippers or carriers as partners in the further development of the Naviporta platform, as they directly add transactions to the platform, we are also conscious of some of the lessons learned from other blockchain consortiums as well as during the Deliver consortium period. For example, while TradeLens was able to quickly ramp up transactional volume on their platform because of Maersk, onboarding of subsequent carriers was slow because of regulatory and business concerns. Swiss carrier MSC’s selection of Wave’s blockchain solution for its global electronic bill of lading (eBL) platform (MSC, 2021), and the establishment of the global blockchain shipping network (GBSN)^([2](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en2)) by among others Hapag Lloyd, COSCO and OOCL, serves as a clear signal of some of the business perils surrounding an intra-industry consortium.

### The perils of cross-industry collaboration

While the regulatory concerns could be circumvented by partnering with companies from different industries for the core, this still leaves the consortium with several challenges. One is them is the limited transactional synergy generated if there’s little or no end-customer overlap. While Samsung SDS and ABN-AMRO are formidable brands, having both their transactions added to the platform resulted in little or no synergy; in other words, 1+1 equalled 2 and not 3. This synergetic outcome is very important when bootstrapping an infrastructure such as Naviporta. Similar holds true when there’s a lot of overlap; processes are more likely to be already tightly integrated, limiting the synergetic effect from an end-customer perspective, as was the case with Samsung SDS and Samsung Electronics. Another important business concern in cross-industry consortia is that parties only have a limited understanding of each other’s businesses. This tend to complicate communication when there are substantial differences in (corporate) cultures. This by no means diminishes the value of cross-industry collaboration, however. We still believe that blockchain is a foundational technology to overcome the challenges of cross-industry collaboration and should be implemented accordingly. Together with the PoR, Blocklab has successfully scaled proof of concepts to spin-outs in collaboration with Standard & Poor’s (SPGlobal, 2020) and British Telecom (Port of Rotterdam, 2021). Just don’t expect to get it right every time at the first try.

### Escaping the mothership(s)

Escaping the gravitational pull of three Deliver motherships (PoR, ABN-AMRO and Samsung SDS) turned out to be challenging and consumed a disproportional amount of resources that could have been put to better use. As explained below, the Deliver project was managed by a steering committee; however, each of the leads had its own mothership ‘shadow’ steering committee to manage. With ABN-AMRO and Samsung SDS gone, that gravitational pull was reduced dramatically. Not only was there now only one mothership left to contend with, but most positions were filled by Blocklab; although a 100 per cent subsidiary of the Port of Rotterdam, it operates at arm’s length of the port and can make decisions autonomously within the budget, provided agreed targets are met.

Based on our experiences within Blocklab we’re now restructuring our approach towards venture building, by making the transition from laboratory project to venture earlier. To start with, this requires identification of the proof of value at the earliest possible stage of the project. Especially for ventures that fall outside the usual line of business, such as is the case for digitization projects for a port authority, establishing a ‘dollars-and-cents’ business case can be challenging, especially in the beginning, as there’s often no base case to fall back on. A simple rule of thumb we have used successfully at Blocklab is to find co-creators willing to invest in kind or in cash as a first validation of the business case. We might think that a certain project is the best invention since sliced bread, but if we cannot convince one or more other companies to put in their resources, it is probably not such a good idea. Also, with time comes knowledge and experience, which allows Blocklab to faster identify pockets of value and their corresponding business case.

Now, asking a corporate to ‘just give us the money and get out of the way’ is not much of a strategy. Also, without the PoR, Blocklab would be nowhere near as successful and Deliver’s founding members’ long-term vision, agenda, brand and network proved extremely valuable early on in the project. However, as the project gains momentum, it is no longer the big corporate logo on the business card that should get you invited to pitch the product, but the product itself! Establishing an own identity for the project can be done in many ways but setting it up as a separate entity with its own management team and budget, and running it like any other start-up, agile, without corporate overhead and skin-in-the-game, gives the best chance to escape the gravitational pull of the motherships. Ideally you want to have already a management team that has done the trick from start-up to scale-up before and covers all the roles, but in most cases a full-time CTO, CCO, CEO and COO for a start-up is overkill. A venture studio, staffed with people who have done the trick before and who can mentor the ideator and the first employees of the start-up, help with finding external funding, grow the business and further build the team, will increase the chances of success.

### If you give a boy a hammer . . .

Blockchain isn’t the solution to all the world’s problems. In effect, when we take a closer look at the architecture of Naviporta (see ‘The key Naviporta components’) we see that off-chain components and the Google Cloud Platform (GCP) play an equally important role in delivering the required functionality. In general, a blockchain transaction requires more processing power than, say, writing a transaction to a database and is therefore more expensive. When designing Naviporta we looked consciously at what transactions required blockchain notarization and which didn’t. Notarization of the ownership of a negotiable electronic bill of lading makes sense as such an asset can represent millions of dollars in value, and the additional cost of notarization pales in comparison. However, notarization of each individual read-out from an Internet-of-Things (IoT) device on board a shipping container is, in most cases, not needed.

## [Naviporta’s whitepaper](#03_toc01.xhtml#fm_toc_048)

At the time of writing the second edition of the book (December 2021), the Naviporta whitepaper was still a work in progress. While this may seem odd as most blockchain whitepapers are written prior to the launch of the protocol, we reasoned that writing a whitepaper earlier would have resulted in a theoretical piece that would have glossed over some of the challenges associated with setting up a consortium blockchain, such as unique selling points (USPs), governance and initial use cases. Furthermore, it would also have been very much PoR’s view on the Naviporta infrastructure. In 2021 we engaged with several port authorities in Asia-Pacific, Australia, Africa and Europe to validate our findings and tighten up the content. This is expected to continue throughout the first quarter of 2022. Therefore, the following paragraphs represent Naviporta’s existing views on several key aspects normally found in a whitepaper, such as vision, core values and governance.

### Vision

In recent years, various digital initiatives emerged on the market that try to solve inefficiencies in today’s global supply chains. Key ports such as Singapore (Wragg, 2021) and Qingdao (GSBN, 2021) are investing in a blockchain-based digital infrastructure, and companies such as freight forwarders, large carriers (eg Maersk and COSCO) and large e-commerce companies (eg Amazon and Alibaba) are building physical and digital infrastructures to support end-to-end supply chains.

A digital infrastructure that takes a cross-industry approach to optimization of global supply chains and has interoperability, neutrality and data sovereignty as its core values will be a powerful instrument to alleviate the many issues supply chains now face as highlighted in [Chapter 2](#05_chapter02.xhtml#ch02_sec_001). This digital infrastructure, utilizing blockchain technology, acts as a common frame of reference over the many siloed systems of record that exist nowadays in global supply chains. As such, it would constitute a foundational infrastructure on which third-party developers and other ecosystems deploy their digital services. This foundational infrastructure acts as a digital highway and is open and accessible for all participants, guaranteeing a level playing field for any actor in global supply chains, taking into account regulatory compliance, eg Know Your Customer (KYC) and Anti Money Laundering (AML) regulation. A wide range of digital services will be offered by various third parties, providing value to end-users (consumers), leveraging the infrastructure to connect data from various services and flows and hence removing inefficiencies and manual handovers. Besides access to all necessary services to facilitate end-to-end supply chain processes, underlying assets and data must be re-usable, eliminating the necessity to recreate and validate the same data at every step in the process, further reducing inefficiencies and data inaccuracies and automating supply chain processes for all parties in the supply chain. The assets and data should all be digital, minimizing the physical paper flow.

Neutrality should be enforced by the governance structure, built around a consortium of, by nature, neutral partners, guaranteeing the neutrality of the infrastructure and providing trust for service providers and end-users to use the platform without fear of vendor lock-in and monetization of their data. Furthermore, the infrastructure should guarantee data-sovereignty, meaning that 1) end-users can always verify the existence, origin and integrity of data, 2) other users of the platform (including Naviporta) are in no way able to access each other’s original data, unless a party has been authorized to do so and 3) consortium partners are not able to create a revenue model based on the end-users’ data. Finally, 4) there should be no infrastructure lock-in, meaning that an end-user can integrate a service from providers outside the platform. The infrastructure should be there for the sole reason to create interoperability and enable ‘connect once, use many’, with the aim to grow cooperation in the ecosystem and optimize the primary processes of the end-users.

### Core values

The Naviporta infrastructure is founded on the following core values:

1.  Data sovereignty: Naviporta stores no master data, business logic and transactional data on the blockchain, apart from the data’s fingerprint (hash). Each user of the blockchain network is empowered to control how their data is used and stored. Hence, Naviporta is not able to monetize any data.
    
2.  Technology agnostic: decentralized apps (Dapp) development; Naviporta doesn’t prescribe any protocol, programming language or proprietary technology to third-party service developers. They are encouraged to develop their services any way they want as long as they adhere to the SDK standards to ensure interoperability with the platform and successfully pass the services’ onboarding process.
    
3.  Enable best-of-breed 2.0: in today’s siloed world even companies that adopt a ‘best-of-breed’ approach when it comes to their supply chain management software must compromise. For example, one electronic bill of lading service provider is stronger in Asia-Pacific, while the other has a stronger presence in Latin-America. Naviporta allows consumers to mix and match their services at a more granular level, even at the shipment level.
    
4.  Neutral: Naviporta is underpinned by a governance structure that doesn’t favour a specific producer, consumer or core ecosystem member. This is achieved by partnering with (port) ecosystems that are committed to the core values and actively grow the partner’s Naviporta ecosystem, by adding new services, consumers, producers and trade lanes.
    

### Ecosystem

While the Naviporta ecosystem still resembles Deliver ecosystems, there has been a fundamental shift towards producers as the key focal point of the Naviporta platform. This is primarily a result of clearly separating the Dapps from the core services as described in the technology paragraph. This has several benefits:

*   core services and Dapps can each have their own business case and governance;
    
*   a clear distinction in operating model: Infrastructure-as-a-Service (IaaS) for the core and Software-as-a-Service (SaaS) for the Dapps;
    
*   enable faster scaling of the platform as producers can potentially on-board members of their own ecosystem that want to make use of other services being offered on the Naviporta platform;
    
*   safeguard the technology agnosticism of both Dapps and core services.
    

In the Deliver era, a tradelane-based strategy between South Korea and the Netherlands was adopted to build the ecosystem, based on the rationale that the three partners were able to mobilize significant traffic on this tradelane alone because of their respective headquarters, the total volume of trade between the two countries and proximity to key stakeholders such as authorities and regulatory bodies. With ABN-AMRO and Samsung SDS no longer part of the consortium, Naviporta had to abandon this tradelane and overall reassess the suitability of the trade lane strategy. As the QuayConnect Dapp (see ‘Supported use cases’) offers tremendous scaling opportunities on those tradelanes connecting the UK, we almost gravitated effortlessly towards a tradelane-based strategy between the European Union (EU) and the United Kingdom (UK).

### Governance

The Naviporta consortium’s main objective is the development and continuity of the Naviporta platform and its core services, the growth of the ecosystem and the assured continuity of the consortium itself. Its governance reflects the core values of Naviporta: neutrality and openness, data sovereignty and interoperability, without compromising efficiency in operations. To design the governance of the Naviporta platform, the governance triangle as stated in [Figure 6.1](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_fig_001) has been used, balancing accountability, decision making model and incentives optimally.

![Figure 6.1 Governance triangle](content/images/M06NF001.jpg)

Figure 6.1 Governance triangle

### Accountability and decision making

As stated, the consortium and platform should be as open as possible, censorship resistant through decentralization and neutrality, but without the risk of becoming inefficient and slow in governance. Taking the governance triangle into account, the schematic overview of the governance of the Naviporta consortium is designed as depicted in [Figure 6.2](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_fig_002).

![Figure 6.2 Naviporta governance](content/images/M06NF002.jpg)

Figure 6.2 Naviporta governance

The proposed design of the governance of the consortium consists of three layers, an accountable layer, a responsible layer and an operational layer.

#### CONSORTIUM

The consortium represents the accountable layer and hence has the highest authority in the consortium. It gathers at least once a year in the general assembly, or more often when called upon by consortium participants or the board (see responsible layer). Decisions are made according to absolute or relative majority, depending on the kind of vote.

Consortium participation is designed to be as open as possible without compromising the neutrality of Naviporta. Therefore, three categories for member submission are determined:

1.  Directly admissible – organizations in this category can become a member of the consortium without the approval of the current participants, as long as they adhere to the minimum requirements for participation (regulatory compliance). These are any (semi-)governmental organization, like port authorities or organizations without any direct profit or commercial interests in the primary processes that the Naviporta platform supports.
    
2.  Excluded organizations – these organizations are allowed to use the Naviporta platform and offer their services via the platform to their customers but cannot become a member of the consortium. These are all organizations that have a direct profit or commercial interest in the primary processes supported by Naviporta and all organizations that are suppliers to the consortium and thus are paid directly by the consortium for their services.
    
3.  To be debated – the third category is for all organizations that cannot be immediately placed in the first or second category. For these organizations to become a member of the consortium, they will need approval from the current participants by majority decision of the consortium at a general assembly.
    

Consortium participants are expected to either run and maintain a Naviporta node or pay a yearly participation fee. Participants receive one vote in the general assembly as a reward.

If a subsidiary of an existing member wants to join the consortium, they won’t receive additional voting rights. This guarantees openness and decentralization on the one hand and neutrality on the other.

The accountable layer is ultimately accountable for all major decisions of the consortium, and it also appoints the board and agrees to the board’s boundaries and year plans. To avoid an inefficient structure, a responsible layer is introduced.

#### BOARD

When the consortium grows beyond six participants, the responsible layer is introduced to guarantee efficiency in governance. The responsible layer is embodied by the board and consists of six participants. The board is appointed by the consortium from among its participants, and can operate freely on a daily basis, within the assigned boundaries, and can delegate the daily operations to the management team. This guarantees efficiency in handling. The main responsibilities are 1) supervision of the management team and 2) managing and maintaining relationships with the observers’ community and reporting to the consortium.

For the appointment of board participants the following criteria will apply: the participants should be geographically spread, meaning that all major continents must be represented. Also, a member of the board may not be affiliated with any of the other participants, to guarantee the strict neutrality of the board.

To guarantee continuity board participants are appointed for three years; every year two board participants are replaced, which minimizes the risk of political re-election bias and thus loss of efficiency as a complete new board needs time to start up – and it reduces the risk that during the last part of their term, the board is more occupied with re-election than their daily tasks.

#### NAVIPORTA OPERATIONS MANAGEMENT TEAM

The daily operations (operational layer) are managed by the management team under the responsibility of the board based on defined key performance indicators. The management team takes care of all the execution of day-to-day tasks, ranging from development of the core infrastructure and software, participation management (KYC, etc) to marketing and sales. The management team is hired by and reports to the board on a weekly basis, and should present any major decisions to the board. The board can decide if the consortium needs to have the final say in the matter (at a general assembly). Those involved in the management team will either be employees of the Naviporta consortium or can be hired from external solution or services providers.

## [Supported use cases](#03_toc01.xhtml#fm_toc_049)

While we envision a diverse ecosystem of consumers, producers and core members, the platform itself is not enough to bootstrap such an ecosystem. To break out of this Catch 22, Naviporta has identified three promising use cases that not only provide direct benefits to the consumers of these services but also show the value of the platform to producers. These services are:

1.  QuayConnect;
    
2.  VATBL0CK;
    
3.  TradeTrust.
    

### QuayConnect

Each year around €360 billion of goods are exported from the EU to the UK. Since Brexit, the UK has sharply tightened the customs formalities, resulting in a strong increase of administrative workload and additional costs for exporting goods to the UK.

Before leaving the EU, goods moved freely between the continent and the UK, with little or no formalities. For example, goods produced or imported into the EU could be sold within the UK without the need for additional health and safety checks at the border of the member country or payment of import duties. On 31 January 2020 the EU and UK agreed on the terms and conditions under which the UK would leave the EU and the new arrangements between the partners in the so called Withdrawal Agreement and Political Declaration. A transition period, keeping most of the pre-Brexit arrangements in place, officially ended on 31 December 2020. On Christmas Eve 2020 the EU and the UK struck a last-minute deal on how to regulate future trade. This new regulation, as laid down in the Border Operating model (UK Government, 2021), constitutes a phased approach towards tariff-free, quota-free access to each other’s markets for goods – but not services. However, the implementation of the model comes with a considerable administrative burden for both exporter and importer. Not only are they required to declare their goods upon import, but also these goods are now subject to more extensive checks from UK customs (HMRC)^([3](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en3)), as well as other authorities, such as Border Force^([4](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en4)) and the Department for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs (DEFRA)^([5](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en5)). As part of this phased approach, new regulation was introduced on 1 January 2022. Among other things, it sees to it that all goods being moved between the EU and the UK now require both an export and an import declaration. The exact implementation differs per port. Add to that the complex rules of origin, the individual trade deals that exist between the UK and the rest of the world, the pandemic and global supply chain issues and it is easy to see why many importers and exporters have trouble in meeting these new custom requirements. Companies that do not meet these complex requirements are faced with delays, additional paperwork and even fines. It was for this reason that a new service called QuayConnect was developed on Naviporta, the neutral blockchain platform for the logistics industry.

#### FIRST SERVICE ON THE NAVIPORTA PLATFORM

QuayConnect is developed in collaboration between Blocklab,^([6](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en6)) Azarc,^([7](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en7)) and the operator of CCS-UK,^([8](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en8)) British Telecom,^([9](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en9)) the Dutch Fresh Produce Association^([10](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en10)) and ABC Logistics.^([11](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en11)) Built on Naviporta, QuayConnect provides a seamless and direct interaction between the system of the exporter, the Dutch port community system (PCS) ‘Portbase’^([12](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en12)) and the UK Cargo Community System (CCS). CCS connects the various members of the cargo community in a digital and distributed way. This community includes HMRC, but also freight forwarders, importers and hauliers. This enables secure, reliable and fast verification of information and creates a seamless and fully automated flow of information between all involved parties, resulting in considerable savings in both time and money. The following steps have been integrated in the solution:

*   preparing, submitting, receiving and handling of the required custom documents in a digital fashion;
    
*   management of the required underlying documentation such as packing list, sales order, etc;
    
*   tracking and tracing of the shipment using an intuitive user interface;
    
*   creating and signing e-CMR, providing immediate digital proof of shipment pick-up and delivery.
    

#### COMMON FRAME OF REFERENCE FOR ALL INVOLVED PARTIES

The blockchain infrastructure supported by Naviporta^([13](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en13)) allows for assets such as documents and datasets originating from the often siloed systems operated by the various cargo community members, to be uniquely registered based on their cryptographically generated hash key, without revealing any sensitive data on the blockchain; it does not require users of the Naviporta infrastructure to store any of their data on the infrastructure, ensuring strict neutrality and making monetization of data by Naviporta impossible. All this results in a common frame of reference over the siloed systems for these assets, which in turn enables the generation of several proofs. These are:

*   Proof of existence – by publishing the hash of the asset on the Naviporta blockchain, the company from which the asset originated can provide proof that this document existed at a given point in time.
    
*   Proof of integrity — when a company shares the underlying asset with a cargo community member, this member can verify whether this asset is the same as the asset that has been registered on the blockchain. Even the smallest change will result in a different hash key for that asset, providing evidence that the asset has been tampered with.
    
*   Proof of origin – this allows authorized users to validate the origin of an asset registered on the Naviporta blockchain.
    
*   Proof of ownership – this allows authorized users to verify who is the owner of a particular asset.
    

Furthermore, various oracles such as Portbase and CCS are connected to QuayConnect, ensuring that validated data is available throughout the various process steps. This in turn facilitates automation of the import customs formalities, providing unprecedented levels of workflow automation, without the need for all the cargo community members to use the same system. The user interface not only provides information about relevant tracking points within the customs declaration process, such as declaration submitted, approved etc, but also whether the goods have been picked up and delivered, improving customer service.

#### THE BUSINESS CASE

Brexit has resulted in additional costs to the tune of €55 million for the Dutch agrifood sector alone. Many additional actions and paperwork have been added to the import and export processes. This has also made the products more expensive for the British customer.

When it comes to the export of perishable goods, speed is crucial. QuayConnect has therefore initially been put to the test by this sector and has been piloted in collaboration with the Dutch Fresh Produce Center (GroentenFruit Huis) and ABC Logistics. Daco Sol, programme manager logistics & supply chain at GroentenFruit Huis, says: ‘QuayConnect is a game changer for the whole industry, but especially for companies that export fresh goods to the UK. This digital service fosters trust, transparency and greater efficiency in the logistics chain that focuses on exports to the UK.’^([14](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en14))

ABC Logistics is one of the first users of the platform. The company exports fruit and vegetables from all around the world to the UK and benefits from a fully automated and digitized flow of documentation. In short, this results in the following advantages:

*   cost savings of at least 30 per cent;
    
*   faster and more efficient processing of documents and goods (at least 20 per cent faster);
    
*   less manual work;
    
*   lower risk of errors;
    
*   better insight into the status of the cargo.
    

QuayConnect is currently live and available as a service on the Naviporta platform.

### VATBL0CK^([15](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en15))

In 2018, the total amount of VAT lost across the EU was estimated at €140 billion (European Commission, 2021). This huge sum of money is also known as the EU VAT gap. One of the core problems that lay at the heart of the VAT gap was fraud and tax evasion. In the literature, blockchain was often mentioned as a technology that could help combat VAT fraud, improve VAT collection mechanisms and therefore tackle the VAT gap.^([16](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en16)) Gruson (Gruson, 2017), for instance, encouraged the European Commission to take the lead and use blockchain as one of the solutions in the efforts towards a single EU VAT area. However, thus far, blockchain has not yet been adopted nor has it been mentioned in the latest EU VAT legislation.^([17](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en17))

While blockchain has not yet been adopted by legislators, it can help companies to become more compliant and in control of their existing VAT compliance obligations – for example, in relation to the correct application of the 0 per cent VAT rate in the case of cross-border business to business (B2B) supplies of goods.^([18](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en18)) In short, suppliers can apply the 0 per cent VAT rate in such transactions if they are able to prove that these goods have left the country of dispatch. In case the correct proof of transport is not obtained, potentially a high financial risk exists, as VAT assessments, penalties and interest can be imposed on the supplier in case of non-compliance.^([19](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en19)) We see in practice that the required paperwork accompanying the current process is a heavy administrative burden for many suppliers.

Until 1 January 2020 the amount of proof needed for the application of the 0 per cent VAT rate differed per EU member state.^([20](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en20)) With the implementation of the ‘VAT quick fixes’ as of that date, simplification and to a certain extent harmonization of these rules has been realized throughout the EU.^([21](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en21)) The new rules include a rebuttable presumption that goods have left the country of dispatch in case the supplier is in possession of at least two pieces of non-contradictory evidence substantiating the transport. If the recipient arranges the transport of the goods, a statement from the recipient is also required. Important to mention is that these pieces of non-contradictory evidence must be issued by two different, independent parties. In addition, the supplier is obliged to have the recipient’s valid VAT identification number. It is crucial that the transport documentation and other evidence can be linked to the invoice.

As a first step, the most obvious benefit is that blockchain could help companies become more compliant for applying the 0 per cent VAT rate by redesigning the supply chain. Given that blockchain is an immutable shared database, it can help to store and trace the movement of goods across the world. This was, for instance, shown by the Naviporta and the TradeLens platform. The Naviporta platform uses IoT devices to track not only the GPS locations of the goods, but also shocks, humidity and if the doors of a container open or close. Each of these data assets is validated and then ‘fingerprinted’ by the Naviporta notary. This similar logic is applied to the e-CMR. This e-CMR is based on the e-protocol that was added to the CMR convention in 2008.^([22](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en22)) This e-protocol provides a legal framework and standards for the use of electronic means to record the CMR consignment note, and has been ratified by nearly all EU countries. For an illustration of how the flow of goods is being recorded on the Naviporta platform, see [Figure 6.3](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_fig_003).

![Figure 6.3 How the flow of goods within the Naviporta platform runs and how meta-data is being stored, immutably, on the blockchain](content/images/M06NF003.jpg)

Figure 6.3 How the flow of goods within the Naviporta platform runs and how meta-data is being stored, immutably, on the blockchain

As well as Naviporta, VATBL0CK is being developed as a solution for companies to fulfil their 0 per cent VAT rate compliance obligations. All meta-data of the required documentation is stored, immutably, on the blockchain. Data integrity is hereby ensured because recorded transactions cannot be changed unless the majority of the network agrees with the changes. Also, data linked together via the blockchain ensures an audit trail, also crucial in case of audits by the tax authorities.

We believe that improving the way suppliers can gather and administer the required evidence (ie the documentation requirements for the application of the 0 per cent VAT rate) is a first step. With the underlying technology maturing and parties getting more familiar with using applications such as VATBL0CK, blockchain could take a more central role within the VAT domain itself. Blockchain could be used as a basis for the application of the 0 per cent VAT rate as blockchain itself (and/or in combination with other technologies, eg IoT, Optical Character Reading (OCR) and other machine learning applications) can ensure proof that goods have been sent or transported from the country of dispatch. In the future, applications’ use of blockchain could ensure compliance by design when it comes to the application of the 0 per VAT rate.

### TradeTrust: digital transfer of ownership of the electronic bill of lading^([23](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en23))

The TradeTrust project envisions the creation of a sandbox environment in which the first legally binding, interoperable, paperless transfer of ownership of an EBL can be conducted on a live shipment. The project kick-off was in April 2019 in Singapore, when delegates from the Maritime Port Authorities (MPA)^([24](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en24)) and the Infocomm Media Development Authority (IMDA),^([25](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en25)) together with the Port of Rotterdam and Blocklab, hosted a two-day workshop with members of Singapore’s maritime, trade and banking community. In October 2019 a technical deep dive was organized around token-enabled digital transfer of ownership. In January 2021, the first TradeTrust-enabled transfer of ownership of an EBL was performed (Port of Rotterdam, 2021).

Since December 2019, the project has been actively supported by the Dutch ministry of economic affairs and climate policy, ICTU, Spark!, LivingLab and the 2Tokens project. TradeTrust is an implementation of the UNCITRAL model law on electronic transferable records (MLETR)^([26](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en26)) and is an active project under UNCEFACT.

TradeTrust is ‘a set of governance & legal frameworks, standards and future-ready digital infrastructure’, facilitating ‘the interoperability of electronic trade documents^([27](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en27)) exchanged between different digital ecosystems’.^([28](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en28))

It does so by separating the responsibility of tracking legal ownership from the data in the document. While the digital document is stored off-chain under the strict control of the original data owner, the ownership thereof can securely be managed on a distributed ledger (in our case Ethereum). Users are free to decide on how they want to digitally exchange the EBL data (eg email, sftp). Anyone in possession of a digital copy of the document can verify the integrity and ownership of the document on the public blockchain.

Each document can be ‘identified uniquely by a document hash’.^([29](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en29)) ^([30](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en30)) TradeTrust documents are based on the OpenAttestation^([31](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en31)) specification, with each document containing ‘information on the schema type, certificate data, hidden data (from privacy filter) and a signature’^([32](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en32)). TradeTrust documents can be serialized to a (.tt) file which is simply a JavaScript Object Notation (JSON) file that conforms to this specification. TradeTrust is payload agnostic; in other words, there are no restrictions on how the document’s content is formatted. In this way interoperability with any existing EBL data exchange formats is ensured. The project is open source and can be used by anybody under the Apache 2.0 licence.

[Figure 6.4](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_fig_004) provides a schematic overview of a TradeTrust implementation.

![Figure 6.4 TradeTrust implementation example](content/images/M06NF004.jpg)

Figure 6.4 TradeTrust implementation example

As mentioned earlier, TradeTrust separates the ownership of an item that a document of title represents from the content of the document. Ownership can be tracked on the blockchain in a secure fashion without storing the document’s data publicly on the blockchain.

TradeTrust tracks the ownership of assets using a smart contract. This smart contract adheres to the ERC-721 token standard,^([33](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en33)) which defines a common software interface for handling ownership of non-fungible tokens. A token is a very large, globally unique number that represents a digital or real world entity, in this case a document of title like the bill of lading. At the core, an ERC-721-compatible contract simply maps token identifiers, or the asset they represent, to the Ethereum address which owns the token and therefore the asset.

Each document in TradeTrust is linked to a non-fungible token on an ERC-721 contract. The link between token and document is created through the hash^([34](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en34)) of the document’s content (the .tt file). The hash is a very large, globally unique number and can be used as the token identifier. A hash acts like a fingerprint of a piece of data: every hash is unique; it is impossible to get back the original data from only the hash and hashing the same data always creates the same hash.

According to TradeTrust’s singularity principle, so long as the token framework is properly implemented, it is ‘practically impossible’^([35](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en35)),^([36](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en36)) for a hash – and therefore the reference to a document – to be duplicated. This is partially due to Ethereum’s necessity for smart contracts to have unique addresses, and partially due to the smart contract itself guaranteeing that an ID can only be registered once in the registry.

ERC-721 smart contracts can be used to track ownership of assets by representing them as a token and assigning an Ethereum address as its owner. However, in many real world applications, ownership is not always that binary. Documents of title, specifically the bill of lading, confer legal asset ownership to a beneficiary. However, the beneficiary is not always the holder of the physical document. To claim the goods, the beneficiary also needs to be in possession of the physical document. So, while the holder does not actually own the assets represented by the document, it can be used as leverage or collateral. Banks often hold the physical documents as collateral, and many trade finance processes rely on this distinction.

The ERC-721 standard does not natively support such advanced ownership constructs. But it does allow other smart contracts to own tokens, as contracts are also represented by Ethereum addresses. TradeTrust has therefore developed the title escrow contract that adds the distinction between holder and beneficiary on top of the core functionality of the ERC-721 contract.

This works as follows: when an EBL is issued – in blockchain terms this boils down to a token being minted – the token is not owned directly by the beneficiary’s Ethereum address. Instead, the issuer also creates a title escrow contract, in which the initial beneficiary and holder (their Ethereum addresses) are named. The token is then owned by the address of the newly created instance of the title escrow contract.

The title escrow contract allows the holder to transfer his rights as a holder to a new address. The holder further has the right to transfer the title to a new Ethereum address, which can again be a new instance of the title escrow contract. This title transfer has to be endorsed by the beneficiary, though. The endorsement through the beneficiary is the equivalent of the beneficiary signing the back of the paper bill of lading.

By handling token ownership in a smart contract, many different ownership constructs are possible, allowing the digital solution to fulfill the required complex constructs that exist in the trade finance world.

Authentication of parties and authorization of actions in the existing paper-based process happens through handwritten signatures. Companies and their employees who are involved in the process will usually have been verified in a KYC process and are often known through long-lasting business relationships. The authenticity and correctness of the information contained in the bill of lading and the signatures are manually verified through large trade-finance teams at the involved banks by looking for any inconsistencies. Although this system has proved itself over the years it is far from efficient and secure. The move to a digital document also allows for improvements of identity verification and ‘compliance by design’.

Although users may want to be anonymous to the public on the blockchain, the involved parties in an international trade transaction will want to identify and verify their counterparts in a transaction. For example, when an issuer mints a token (which is equivalent to the carrier issuing the paper bill of lading) it is passed on to the *Address* of the shipper. The issuer will want to verify that the *Address* that he got from the contact (through email for example) is actually controlled by the shipper (usually the supplier of the goods) company or even an authorized representative of that company. In the same way that the title transfer should be possible without depending on third parties, this verification should be possible without involving a third party. The shipper’s employee should simply be able to prove to the issuer who he or she is in a privacy preserving way. This is possible using decentralized identifiers (DID) and verifiable credentials (VC), two data standards recently published by the W3C.

DIDs are merely a way to identify an entity globally and uniquely, like a bar code identifying a certain product. DIDs can be implemented with many technologies but when talking specifically about this EBL solution on Ethereum the DID would use the Ethereum *Address* as identifier. To attach additional information for verification to a DID, a verifiable credential can be issued with the DID as its subject. A credential could state anything about the subject, eg age, a driver’s licence or school certificate. Also, credentials are meant to be issuable by anyone or anything; often an issuer would also have a DID. Furthermore, as the name suggests, VC are designed in a way that they can be verified. As the issuer creates a cryptographic signature on the credential, anyone in possession of the signed credential can also verify its authenticity. [Figure 6.5](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_fig_005) shows the process of obtaining and presenting credentials.

![Figure 6.5 Information flow verifiable credential (W3C, 2021)](content/images/M06NF005.jpg)

Figure 6.5 Information flow verifiable credential (W3C, 2021)

Going back to the example where the carrier would need to verify the identity of a shipper we can explore how verifiable credentials can help in this situation. To prove to the carrier that the Ethereum *Address* receiving the EBL token is controlled by an authorized employee, the controller of the *Address* would need a credential. Such a credential could, for example, be issued by the Chamber of Commerce. That means, before the issuance of the EBL, the shipper’s employee obtains a cryptographically signed verifiable credential, stating that she is an employee of the shipper and authorized to sign contracts. The shipper sends that credential to the carrier’s clerk, who can verify the correctness of the cryptographic signature. The clerk also needs to make sure that the issuer of the credential is the Chamber of Commerce. This requires the Chamber of Commerce to publish its DID. If the credential passes the validation through the carrier’s clerk, and the Chamber of Commerce can be trusted, there is no doubt that the Ethereum *Address* must be controlled by an authorized employee of the shipper and the clerk can proceed with the issuance of the EBL token to the verified *Address*.

There are a few requirements for the exchange of credentials to work. As mentioned, the DID of the Chamber of Commerce, which issues the credentials, needs to be well known and trusted. The verifier should only accept credentials from issuers they trust. Although credentials can be issued by anyone, credentials from certain issuers have more credibility than others. Also, verifier and issuer need to agree on a standardized data format for the credentials they use. If the issuer would use a different format, the verifier might not accept it and the credential would be rendered worthless for the holder. This requires some form of standardization or communication between issuer, holder and verifier.

### Our technology

We believe our vision, core values and governance set us apart from other (blockchain) platforms. These unique characteristics are reflected in our current and future-state architecture and our technology choices. Before we describe the various on-chain and off-chain components, we will first delve deeper into why we made the choice of the Ethereum protocol.

#### WHY USE THE ETHEREUM EVM?

To avoid spending valuable resources on reinventing the wheel and to ensure that we keep our eye on developing solutions that solve real world problems, we made a conscious decision not to develop our own protocol. Instead, we decided to use the Golang implementation of the Ethereum Virtual Machine (EVM), the so-called Geth client. While we keep our implementation of the client fully compatible with the Geth client as can be found on the Geth Ethereum download page^([37](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en37)), Naviporta is not Ethereum, but is a different technology stack and a different chain. By steering clear from making changes on the (Geth) client level we can ensure compatibility of our chain with the Ethereum mainnet and migrate to the public, permissionless chain when this becomes opportune.

But what were the reasons to choose Ethereum above other protocols? First and foremost, it is the most popular open source public blockchain protocol, with an ever-growing development community and open-source development tools and extensions. Other protocols such as IOTA^([38](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en38)) and Solana^([39](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en39)) now allowing for EVM-compatible smart contracts to run on their native chains only strengthens that position. Second, the EVM is so-called Turing complete, which means that it can handle any form of application logic, as found in common programming languages such as Java and C++. Third, the mainnet has been battle tested since 2015 and has proved itself to be resilient. Fourth, we keep the option open to migrate to the public, permissionless Ethereum mainnet if this is required from a business perspective. And finally, the development team at Blocklab is highly experienced in the use of the EVM.

Is Ethereum perfect? The short answer is ‘no, it isn’t!’. But we found ways around its imperfections without having to make changes at a client level. Two of the imperfections deserve some more attention. First, the Ethereum mainnet is often criticized for its low scalability, with transactions per second between 15 and 20 TPS, while large centralized applications such as Alipay or Mastercard can process hundreds of thousands of transactions per second. One important factor contributing to that is the proof of work consensus algorithm, used by the mainnet. As we choose to have our own chain that, while compatible with the EVM, runs independently from the mainnet, we were able to adopt another consensus algorithm. We choose the Clique proof of authority consensus mechanism (Szilágyi, 2017). Tests have shown that we can scale to 500~1000 TPS while still making use of the existing Google cloud infrastructure. Also, EVM compatible protocols such as Avalanche^([40](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en40)) and Solana offer scalability in the range of 3000 TPS to 4500 TPS, providing potential alternatives in the case of further scaling being needed.

Second, the increasing value and price volatility of Ethers, and other publicly traded cryptocurrencies such as Bitcoin, is making transactions very expensive. And although some publicly traded companies do now have Bitcoins on their balance sheet (Bitcoin Magazine, 2021), most corporates today steer clear of utilizing them. By deploying our own chain, we were able to circumvent the use of cryptocurrency as we do not need to reward the public miners for generating a block or performing a transaction. To remain compatible with the EVM we did generate Naviporta coins, but these coins currently have no value and are not publicly traded. Their usage is to keep the existing logic of how transactions are paid for on the Ethereum mainnet intact, to prevent having to make changes to the EVM. Also, the computing power required for running the Clique consensus algorithm is much less than for proof of work or proof of stake protocols. This reduces energy consumption, and further lowers the operating costs for the validators.

### The key Naviporta components

[Figure 6.6](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_fig_006) captures the existing components of the Naviporta infrastructure.

![Figure 6.6 Naviporta and QuayConnect components](content/images/M06NF006.jpg)

Figure 6.6 Naviporta and QuayConnect components

We will start with a description of the so-called core components, ie those components that are foundational to the network itself and cannot be replaced by a similar component offered by another third party. Note that this doesn’t mean that these Naviporta components are not interoperable with other similar components, deployed on other infrastructures and networks, it is just that by replacing them with another third-party component the essence of Naviporta as an infrastructure would be subverted. After that we will describe the infrastructure services. These services, while currently being offered by Naviporta, can be replaced by offerings from a third party.

#### CORE COMPONENTS

Currently the blockchain network utilizes the Google Cloud Platform (GCP) and the signer nodes are deployed over different geographical regions. Each node contains a full copy of the blockchain ledger. In the previous section we discussed at some length the underlying protocol and the use of the EVM.

We also briefly touched upon the Clique proof of authority consensus algorithm. Such a proof of authority scheme is based on the idea that blocks may only be minted by trusted signers. The responsibility of creating the next block is shared among a set of authorities and is done in a round-robin fashion (Polarsparc, 2020). As more consumers and producers onboard on the infrastructure, more sophisticated and less predictable block validation schedules could be implemented. We are currently examining various technical and non-technical mitigation strategies.

Each block header contains the digital signature of the miner of that block, which allows anyone obtaining a block to verify it against a list of authorized signers (Szilágyi, 2017). New or obsolete signers can be added or removed to maintain a dynamic, up-to-date list of signers. While we are aware of some of the limitations of the proof of authority consensus, we believe that by the way we have organized the governance of the Naviporta chain, the decision-making power is distributed over a geographically dispersed and neutral group of network participants, ie ports. The criteria for becoming a consortium member are also fully transparent and we actively seek to further expand the consortium over the next two years to increase its geographical distribution. This increases the difficulty of a coordinated distributed denial of service (DDoS) attack on signers; an often-voiced criticism of proof of authority consortium blockchains.

The core also includes an asset registry that can be used by notaries on the service layer to register assets. The main responsibility of the asset registry is to keep a mapping between assets and their origin and current managing notary. The managing notary is the notary that currently manages the asset on behalf of the owner. This registry serves the following:

1.  Someone that receives an asset can use the registry to find the current managing notary and request meta-data from it through the notary.
    
2.  It allows the notary that initially registered the asset to be retrieved (proof of origin/existence).
    
3.  It allows for asset tracking even when it has been transferred to multiple notaries.
    
4.  It gives Naviporta the possibility to trace back the history of any asset registered on the blockchain ledger in case of problems, bugs, clean-up or a notary producer default.
    
5.  It can act as a single registration source that producers can use to ensure an asset is only ‘registered/used’ once. For example, a negotiable bill of lading that acts as collateral against debt, such as a letter of credit.
    

On the service level there can be many notaries that share the same asset registry. These notaries can add specific business logic, such as the transfer of asset ownership when some condition is met, or shared ownership by a consortium of participants, and have a commercial purpose.

The other Naviporta core service is the identity provider. Not only will Naviporta have many parties and users collaborating and consequently providing services on the infrastructure that require authentication and identification, but also machines, for example oracles or IoT. This means that authentication and identifications are very important aspects and offered by the core to avoid any hassle in authenticating each party. Identification and authentication can get very complex very quickly on an infrastructure such as Naviporta. For example, a user representing a certain company creates a shipment in a third-party application, such as QuayConnect (see ‘QuayConnect’) and wants to make use of an eCMR service that is offered by another third party. The latter requires that company and/or user-specific data must be shared between two services that each have their own identity provider. Of course, the user does not want to log in separately on each identity provider. Taking this one step further, the eCMR is stored in a digital vault offered by yet another service provider. To ensure seamless authentication and identification, a single sign-on is required that offers on-behalf-authentication to enable the QuayConnect application that the user has logged into and uses the data received on authentication to request data at the vault on behalf of the user in a secure and traceable manner.

The identity provider makes use of the OpenID Connect^([41](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en41)) API to allow users to authenticate themselves at a service. As a result of the successful authentication a special token will be provided to the requesting party. This special token contains several claims and proofs according to the W3C verifiable credentials^([42](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en42)) specification. One of the claims will be the corporate ID claim, which will be provided by the corporate ID CA and will bind the signing key of the identity provider for the other claims with the corporate ID and company details. Verifiable credentials are explained in more detail in the ‘TradeTrust’ section below. An HTTP API and a frontend is available for (admin) users to manage the identity provider details (add users, reset passwords, change user details, etc).

#### INFRASTRUCTURE SERVICES

The vault is used to securely store assets. This can be documents, but also non-structured datasets, on behalf of the owning company of the asset, and allows sharing it with other parties. It receives the assets from different sources such as uploads from a Dapp, such as QuayConnect or data produced by an oracle. An asset is uploaded using an HTTP file and stored in Google Storage. A record in the asset table is created that holds ownership, filename, content type, size, upload timestamp and optional auxiliary meta-data. For each uploaded asset a universally unique identifier (UUID version 4^([43](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en43))) is generated that acts as a surrogate key. In addition, this UUID has no context allowing it to be used in the notary without revealing any information about the asset or owner to satisfy a privacy requirement that prevents any leakage of information. Next to the asset itself, the vault supports two types of meta-data that can be stored for an asset:

*   Meta-data stored in the database that is associated with the vault; this holds (semi-) private data and is only accessible by users that are authorized to download the asset itself. An authorization matrix ensures that access can be managed.
    
*   Meta-data registered on the notary, which is immutable and verifiable by anyone. Useful for irrefutable claims/proofs at the cost of publishing it on to the network.
    

The uploader can request the vault to notarize the document giving a proof of existence at a certain time. The architecture supports the use of multiple vaults, and a consumer is free to pick a vault to be used for storing the data they own. The use of multiple vaults further strengthens the distributed nature of the infrastructure.

The shipment registry keeps track of the shipments defined for a certain customer in the network and the assets attached to them. The definition of a shipment is flexible: it is defined as a graph with multiple nodes and relations between them. Each node defines some unit of transport (shipment, container, pallet, truck, etc) with a type and one or more IDs. Data about what is inside the shipment is not stored in the shipment registry. This can only be retrieved from the vault by an authorized user. An example of such a node is a container with a container ID. In this container multiple pallets can be shipped that also need to be tracked individually. These pallets are then also nodes in the shipment registry and have a relation to the container that is modelled as a relation between the nodes. Each node can have multiple assets assigned to it, to track the assets that are related to that shipment. The shipment registry allows sharing the IDs and assets related to a shipment so others can find them. This ensures that data about, for example, a container can be shared between multiple Dapps and as such supports the ‘connect once, use many’ philosophy of the Naviporta infrastructure. The architecture supports multiple shipment registries to co-exist. A consumer can pick a shipment registry to be used for storing the data they own.

A notary provides a trusted third-party function in the network notarizing some proof. The most basic example is the proof of existence (in time). Later, more complicated notaries can exist, for example shared ownership. These notaries are commercial services and part of the service layer. A good example is TradeTrust, which is a specific notary that provides proof of ownership, proof of integrity and proof of origin related to the transfer of electronic records, such as a negotiable electronic bill of lading.

Oracles are implemented using the oracle SDK (see the section below) and as such act as a bridge between the blockchain infrastructure and data from the outside world. There could potentially be thousands of oracles connected to the infrastructure, and the architecture supports oracles from different suppliers to co-exist and provide similar data. At the time of writing the following oracles are available or in the process of being developed:

*   Portbase, the port community system of the ports in the Netherlands. Portbase provides validated data about the tracking points associated with import of containers, such as date/time of arrival of the vessel and gate-out and customs statuses.
    
*   Transfollow provides an eCMR service. An eCMR is similar to a digital waybill and is mandatory for all cross-border road transport within the EU. This service provides validated data about date/time of pick-up and delivery as well as location.
    
*   CCS and AIS^([44](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en44)) provide services related to the sharing of data with the trade community in the UK, including the data necessary to (pre)lodge customs clearance declarations with HMRC.
    

#### SOFTWARE DEVELOPMENT KITS (SDK)

Naviporta has developed several software development kits (SDKs) to ease development and prevent repetitive code for the following core and services components:

*   vault;
    
*   identity;
    
*   oracle;
    
*   notary.
    

These SDKs will be made available under an open source licence to third parties who wish to develop services on top of the Naviporta infrastructure or integrate external systems of record, such as ERP and PCS. SDKs help in simplifying and lowering the overall cost of integration.

The SDKs include the following functionality:

*   A definition of shared models such as identity, assets or shipments.
    
*   HTTP middleware library that includes functionality to verify credentials, metrics and logging.
    
*   Transaction management functionality; sending a transaction to a decentralized system is not as simple as ‘fire and forget’. For example, a malfunctioning node or network latency can cause a transaction never to be broadcast on to the network or to only reach part of the network. Also, as proof of authority, consensus algorithms rely heavily on timestamp accuracy and synchronization, small issues with timestamps might result in (temporary) forks. The SDKs offer built-in support for common transaction management challenges as can be encountered when interacting with decentralized infrastructure.
    
*   Documentation and definitions of application programming interfaces (APIs).
    
*   Documentation and definitions and smart contract-application binary interfaces (ABIs).^([45](#09_chapter06.xhtml#ch06_en45))
    
*   Standards for liveness and readiness probes in the Kubernetes cluster to control the health of the component.
    
*   Standards for loading runtime configuration of services such as the vault and shipment registry.
    
*   A standard method for handling encrypted assets and materials.
